Let’s go step by step.  

---

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**  
The problem statement only gives the source code and asks for an optimized version, but the “optimized code” itself is not provided here — only its function signature `s281_opt`.  
Since we cannot see the actual optimized code, we cannot check for compilation errors directly.  
But the feedback says: *“Unit Test analysis: Source code and optimized code semantics are inconsistent.”*  
That means the optimized code compiled (otherwise unit tests wouldn’t run), but the output is wrong.  

So we proceed to **semantic comparison**.

---

**2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**  

**Source code logic:**  
For each iteration `nl`, loop `i = 0..LEN_1D-1`:
```
x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
Important: `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is **read** from the original `a` (or updated earlier in this iteration of `nl` depending on `i`).  
Let’s check dependencies:

- `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` read:  
  When `i` is small, `LEN_1D - i - 1` is large → accesses end of array.  
  When `i` is large, `LEN_1D - i - 1` is small → accesses beginning of array.  
  So in the same `nl` iteration, earlier `i` updates `a[i]` (low indices), which will be read later when `i` is large enough that `LEN_1D - i - 1` equals that earlier index.  

Example: `LEN_1D = 4`  
`i=0`: read `a[3]` (original), write `a[0]`  
`i=1`: read `a[2]` (original), write `a[1]`  
`i=2`: read `a[1]` (just updated at `i=1` in same `nl` iteration! not original)  
`i=3`: read `a[0]` (updated at `i=0` in same `nl` iteration)  

So there is a **true dependency** between iterations of the inner loop:  
`a[i]` written at iteration `i` is read at iteration `LEN_1D - i - 1` (if `LEN_1D - i - 1 > i`, that’s later in the loop). Wait — check:  
`LEN_1D - i - 1 > i` → `LEN_1D > 2i + 1` → for `i < (LEN_1D - 1)/2`, the read index is larger than `i`, so it hasn’t been overwritten yet in this `nl` iteration.  
Actually, let’s map carefully:

Take `i=0`, read `a[3]` (not yet written in this `nl`).  
`i=1`, read `a[2]` (not yet written).  
`i=2`, read `a[1]` (was written at `i=1` earlier in this `nl`).  
`i=3`, read `a[0]` (was written at `i=0` earlier in this `nl`).  

So for `i >= ceil(LEN_1D/2)`, the `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` was already updated earlier in this `nl` iteration.  
Thus **within the same `nl` iteration**, there is a loop-carried dependency:  
- Write to `a[i]` at iteration `i`  
- Read from `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` at iteration `LEN_1D - i - 1`  
These are symmetric: if `j = LEN_1D - i - 1`, then iteration `j` writes `a[j]` which is read at iteration `i`.  
So each pair `(i, LEN_1D - i - 1)` has a dependency in opposite order in the loop.

Thus the loop **cannot be reordered** arbitrarily; reversing the loop or parallelizing without care will break semantics.

---

**3. Likely cause of semantic inconsistency in optimized code**  
The optimized code probably tried to vectorize or change loop order, breaking this symmetric dependency.  
For example, if it split the loop into two separate loops (one for first half, one for second half), the second half’s reads of `a[first half]` would see the updated values from the same `nl` iteration, which is correct, but if the first half’s reads of `a[second half]` see original values (not yet updated), that’s also correct.  
But if they reordered such that all writes happen before all reads in the same `nl`, that would be wrong.

Given unit test says semantics inconsistent, the optimized code is **wrong**.

---

**4. Vectorization analysis**  
Since semantics are already wrong, we stop here. The optimized code must first be fixed to match source semantics.

---

**Conclusion:**  
The optimized code fails semantic equivalence due to breaking the loop-carried dependency between symmetric indices in the same `nl` iteration.  
No vectorization is possible without first ensuring correctness.

---

UnitTest Feedback:

Unit Test analysis: 
Source code and optimized code semantics are inconsistent.


**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
## Analysis Report  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code  
The optimized code compiles without errors.  

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases  
The optimized code attempts to split the loop into two halves to resolve dependencies, but it introduces a **semantic error**.  

**Dependency analysis**:  
In the original loop, for iteration `i`, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is read. When `i` is small, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` corresponds to a high index in `a`. In the second half of the loop (`i >= LEN_1D/2`), `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` refers to indices that were already updated in the **first half** of the same `nl` iteration.  

The optimized code splits the loop into two halves, assuming:  
- First half: `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is **not updated** yet in this `nl` iteration.  
- Second half: `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is **already updated** in the first half.  

However, this assumption is **incorrect** for indices near the middle. Let’s test with a small example:  

**Example**: `LEN_1D = 4`, initial `a = [A0, A1, A2, A3]`, `b = [B0, B1, B2, B3]`, `c = [C0, C1, C2, C3]`.  

**Original code** (`iterations = 1`):  
- `i=0`: `x = a[3] + B0*C0`, update `a[0]`, `b[0]`.  
- `i=1`: `x = a[2] + B1*C1`, update `a[1]`, `b[1]`.  
- `i=2`: `x = a[1] + B2*C2`, update `a[2]`, `b[2]`.  
- `i=3`: `x = a[0] + B3*C3`, update `a[3]`, `b[3]`.  

**Optimized code** (`half = 2`):  
First half (`i=0,1`):  
- `i=0`: `x = a[3] + B0*C0`, update `a[0]`, `b[0]`.  
- `i=1`: `x = a[2] + B1*C1`, update `a[1]`, `b[1]`.  

Second half (`i=2,3`):  
- `i=2`: `x = a[1] + B2*C2` → **Here `a[1]` was updated in first half**, but in original it was `a[1]` (old value).  
- `i=3`: `x = a[0] + B3*C3` → **Here `a[0]` was updated in first half**, but in original it was `a[0]` (old value).  

Thus, the optimized code uses **updated values** of `a[1]` and `a[0]` in the second half, while the original uses **old values**. This changes the semantics.  

**Conclusion**: The optimized code is **semantically incorrect**.  

### 3. Vectorization Analysis  
Since the semantics are incorrect, vectorization analysis is not applicable.  

### 4. Optimization Opportunity  
The original loop has a **loop-carried anti-dependency** (read `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` before it is overwritten later in the same iteration for some `i`). This prevents vectorization.

void s281_opt(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a,float* b,float* c)
{
    float x;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // First half: i < LEN_1D/2
        // For these iterations, a[LEN_1D-i-1] hasn't been updated yet in this nl iteration
        int half = LEN_1D/2;
        for (int i = 0; i < half; i++) {
            x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Second half: i >= LEN_1D/2
        // For these iterations, a[LEN_1D-i-1] was already updated in first half
        for (int i = half; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;

The optimized code attempts to split the loop into two halves to resolve dependencies, but it introduces a **semantic error**.

In the original loop, for iteration `i`, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is read. When `i` is small, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` corresponds to a high index in `a`. In the second half of the loop (`i >= LEN_1D/2`), `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` refers to indices that were already updated in the **first half** of the same `nl` iteration.  

The optimized code splits the loop into two halves, assuming:  
- First half: `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is **not updated** yet in this `nl` iteration.  
- Second half: `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is **already updated** in the first half.  

However, this assumption is **incorrect** for indices near the middle. Let’s test with a small example:

- `i=1`: `x = a[2] + B1*C1`, update `a[1]`, `b[1]`.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;

- i=1: x = a_old[1] + 2 = 20 + 2 = 22 → b[1] = 22

a[0]=20, a[1]=11

The optimized code assumes all reads from `a` are from the start-of-iteration state, which is wrong.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**2. Semantic comparison**  

The original code in each iteration does:  

```
x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0;
b[i] = x;
```

The optimized code splits into:  

First loop:  
```
b[i] = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
```  
Second loop:  
```
a[i] = b[i] - 1.0f;
```  

But note: In the original, when computing `x` for index `i`, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is read from the **original** `a` array from the previous iteration of `nl` (or initial state if first `nl`). However, in the optimized version, the first loop reads `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` which may have already been overwritten in the **current** `nl` iteration for indices `j` where `LEN_1D-j-1 = i` earlier in the loop.  

Let’s test with a small example:  

**Example 1:**  
`LEN_1D = 3`, initial:  
a = [a0, a1, a2]  
b = [b0, b1, b2]  
c = [c0, c1, c2]  

Original loop `i=0`:  
x0 = a[2] + b0*c0 = a2 + b0*c0  
a[0] = x0 - 1  
b[0] = x0  

`i=1`:  
x1 = a[1] + b1*c1  (a[1] still original a1)  
a[1] = x1 - 1  
b[1] = x1  

`i=2`:  
x2 = a[0] + b2*c2  (a[0] is **new** x0-1 from current iteration)  
a[2] = x2 - 1  
b[2] = x2  

So `a[0]` is updated before being read for `i=LEN_1D-1` in the same `nl` iteration.  

---

Optimized version:  

First loop `i=0`:  
b[0] = a[2] + b0*c0  

`i=1`:  
b[1] = a[1] + b1*c1  

`i=2`:  
b[2] = a[0] + b2*c2  ← **Here a[0] is still original a0** (not updated yet because a updates happen in second loop).  

Second loop:  
a[0] = b[0] - 1  
a[1] = b[1] - 1  
a[2] = b[2] - 1  

Difference: In original, when `i=2`, `a[0]` used is `x0-1` (updated in same iteration). In optimized, when `i=2`, `a[0]` used is original `a0`.  

Thus semantics differ.  

---

**3. Conclusion**  
The optimized code changes the semantics because the first loop reads `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` from the state before any updates to `a` in this `nl` iteration, while the original reads `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` which may have been updated earlier in the same iteration (when `LEN_1D-i-1 < i`).  

Since unit test analysis already indicates inconsistency, we stop here.

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**  
The optimized code uses `malloc` and `free` but does not include `<stdlib.h>`.  
In C, without `<stdlib.h>`, `malloc` and `free` are implicitly declared with return type `int`, which is incorrect for `float*`.  
This would cause a compilation error unless the compiler is in a mode that tolerates implicit declarations (C99 and later forbid it by default).  
Thus, **the optimized code fails to compile** in a strict C environment.  

---

**2. Semantic comparison (if we ignore compilation error)**  
Even if we fix the missing header, the semantics are **different** from the source code.  

**Source code loop body**:  
```
x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0;
b[i] = x;
```

In the source, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` in iteration `i` is the **original** `a` element from the opposite end of the array **at the start of the iteration**, but note:  
- For `i` from `0` to `LEN_1D-1`, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is being read **before** it is overwritten in iteration `LEN_1D-i-1`.  
- Wait, check carefully:  
  Iteration `j = LEN_1D-i-1` corresponds to iteration `i = LEN_1D-j-1`.  
  In iteration `i`, we read `a[LEN_1D-i-1]`.  
  That element `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` will be written in iteration `j = LEN_1D-i-1`.  
  But when does iteration `j` happen relative to iteration `i`?  
  If `i < LEN_1D-i-1`, then iteration `i` happens **before** iteration `j`, so the read uses the original `a` value from before the loop.  
  If `i > LEN_1D-i-1`, then iteration `j` has already occurred, so the read uses the **updated** `a` value from iteration `j`.  

Thus there is a **loop-carried dependency** in the source code:  
`a[i]` in iteration `i` depends on `a[LEN_1D-i-1]`, which may have been updated earlier in the same loop if `LEN_1D-i-1 < i`.  

---

**Example**: `LEN_1D = 3`, original `a = [a0, a1, a2]`, `b = [b0, b1, b2]`, `c = [c0, c1, c2]`.  

Source execution:  
i=0: read a[2] (original a2), x = a2 + b0*c0, a[0] = x-1, b[0] = x  
i=1: read a[1] (original a1), x = a1 + b1*c1, a[1] = x-1, b[1] = x  
i=2: read a[0] (now a[0] is updated from i=0!), x = (a2+b0*c0-1) + b2*c2, a[2] = x-1, b[2] = x

i=0: b0 = a_orig[2] + b0*c0 = a2 + b0*c0  
i=1: b1 = a_orig[1] + b1*c1 = a1 + b1*c1  
i=2: b2 = a_orig[0] + b2*c2 = a0 + b2*c2  
Loop2: a[i] = b[i] - 1.0  
a = [b0-1, b1-1, b2-1]  

Compare final `a[2]` after one nl iteration:  
Source: a[2] = (a2+b0*c0-1) + b2*c2 - 1 = a2 + b0*c0 + b2*c2 - 2  
Optimized: a[2] = b2 - 1 = a0 + b2*c2 - 1  

These are **different** unless `a0 = a2 + b0*c0 - 1`, which is not generally true.  
Thus semantics differ.  

---

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

---

### **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**
The optimized code compiles without errors (no syntax issues, includes proper headers, uses `malloc`/`free` correctly).

### **2. Semantic comparison**

**Source code logic per iteration `nl`**:

For each `i` from `0` to `LEN_1D-1`:
```
x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
Important: `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is **original `a` from before the loop iteration `i`** in the current `nl` iteration, but note:  
- When `i < LEN_1D/2`, `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is from the **second half** of `a`, which hasn’t been overwritten yet in this `nl` iteration.  
- When `i >= LEN_1D/2`, `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is from the **first half** of `a`, which **has already been overwritten** earlier in this same `nl` iteration (since we write to `a[i]` earlier in the loop).  
Thus, the source code has a **loop-carried dependency** through `a` within the same `nl` iteration.

---

**Optimized code logic per iteration `nl`**:

1. Copy `a` to `a_copy`.
2. Compute `x = a_copy[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i]` and store in `b[i]`.
3. Update `a[i] = b[i] - 1.0f`.

This uses `a_copy[LEN_1D - i - 1]` from **original `a` at start of `nl` iteration**, not from partially updated `a` within the loop.  
This differs from source semantics when `i >= LEN_1D/2`.

---

**Example to show difference**:

Let `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 1`, initial values:  
`a = [10, 20, 30]`, `b = [1, 2, 3]`, `c = [1, 1, 1]`.

**Source code** (`nl=0`):

i=0: `x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0] = 30 + 1 = 31` → `a[0]=30`, `b[0]=31`  
i=1: `x = a[1] + b[1]*c[1] = 20 + 2 = 22` → `a[1]=21`, `b[1]=22`  
i=2: `x = a[0] + b[2]*c[2] = 30 + 3 = 33` → `a[2]=32`, `b[2]=33`  
Final `a = [30, 21, 32]`, `b = [31, 22, 33]`.

**Optimized code** (`nl=0`):

`a_copy = [10, 20, 30]`  
Loop1:  
i=0: `x = a_copy[2] + 1 = 31` → `b[0]=31`  
i=1: `x = a_copy[1] + 2 = 22` → `b[1]=22`  
i=2: `x = a_copy[0] + 3 = 13` → `b[2]=13`  
Loop2:  
`a[i] = b[i] - 1` → `a = [30, 21, 12]`.

Compare:  
Source `a = [30, 21, 32]`, optimized `a = [30, 21, 12]` → mismatch at `a[2]`.  
Reason: In source, `i=2` uses `a[0]` which was updated in same `nl` iteration to `30`, but optimized uses `a_copy[0] = 10`.

---

Thus, **semantics are inconsistent**. The optimized code fails to preserve the loop-carried dependency on `a` within the same iteration.

Source code: 
```c
void s281(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a,float* b,float* c)
{
    float x;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x-(float)1.0;
            b[i] = x;
        }
    }
}
```

Optimized code:
```c

#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>

void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    float* a_copy = (float*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Copy 'a' to preserve original values for reverse access
        memcpy(a_copy, a, LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
        
        // Vectorizable loop: compute all x values using original 'a' from copy
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            float x = a_copy[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Vectorizable loop: update 'a' using computed 'b'
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            a[i] = b[i] - 1.0f;
        }
    }
    free(a_copy);
}

```

Feedback:
Let’s analyze step-by-step.

---

### **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**
The optimized code compiles without errors (no syntax issues, includes proper headers, uses `malloc`/`free` correctly).

---

### **2. Semantic comparison**

**Source code logic per iteration `nl`**:

For each `i` from `0` to `LEN_1D-1`:
```
x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
Important: `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is **original `a` from before the loop iteration `i`** in the current `nl` iteration, but note:  
- When `i < LEN_1D/2`, `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is from the **second half** of `a`, which hasn’t been overwritten yet in this `nl` iteration.  
- When `i >= LEN_1D/2`, `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is from the **first half** of `a`, which **has already been overwritten** earlier in this same `nl` iteration (since we write to `a[i]` earlier in the loop).  
Thus, the source code has a **loop-carried dependency** through `a` within the same `nl` iteration.

---

**Optimized code logic per iteration `nl`**:

1. Copy `a` to `a_copy`.
2. Compute `x = a_copy[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i]` and store in `b[i]`.
3. Update `a[i] = b[i] - 1.0f`.

This uses `a_copy[LEN_1D - i - 1]` from **original `a` at start of `nl` iteration**, not from partially updated `a` within the loop.  
This differs from source semantics when `i >= LEN_1D/2`.

---

**Example to show difference**:

Let `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 1`, initial values:  
`a = [10, 20, 30]`, `b = [1, 2, 3]`, `c = [1, 1, 1]`.

**Source code** (`nl=0`):

i=0: `x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0] = 30 + 1 = 31` → `a[0]=30`, `b[0]=31`  
i=1: `x = a[1] + b[1]*c[1] = 20 + 2 = 22` → `a[1]=21`, `b[1]=22`  
i=2: `x = a[0] + b[2]*c[2] = 30 + 3 = 33` → `a[2]=32`, `b[2]=33`  
Final `a = [30, 21, 32]`, `b = [31, 22, 33]`.

**Optimized code** (`nl=0`):

`a_copy = [10, 20, 30]`  
Loop1:  
i=0: `x = a_copy[2] + 1 = 31` → `b[0]=31`  
i=1: `x = a_copy[1] + 2 = 22` → `b[1]=22`  
i=2: `x = a_copy[0] + 3 = 13` → `b[2]=13`  
Loop2:  
`a[i] = b[i] - 1` → `a = [30, 21, 12]`.

Compare:  
Source `a = [30, 21, 32]`, optimized `a = [30, 21, 12]` → mismatch at `a[2]`.  
Reason: In source, `i=2` uses `a[0]` which was updated in same `nl` iteration to `30`, but optimized uses `a_copy[0] = 10`.

---

Thus, **semantics are inconsistent**. The optimized code fails to preserve the loop-carried dependency on `a` within the same iteration.

---

### **3. Conclusion**
Since semantics differ, we stop here. The optimized code is incorrect.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

## **2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases**

The original loop accesses `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` in iteration `i`.  
In the original code, `a` is being updated in the same loop, so later iterations reading `a[LEN_1D - j - 1]` may read a value that has already been updated by an earlier iteration if `LEN_1D - j - 1` is less than `j`.  
This happens when `i < LEN_1D/2` roughly:  
- For `i = 0`, we read `a[LEN_1D - 1]` (original value).  
- For `i = LEN_1D - 1`, we read `a[0]`, which by then has been updated in iteration `i = 0`.

Thus, there is a **cross-iteration dependency** between `a[i]` and `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]`.

The optimized code tries to split the loop into two halves:  
- First half (`i < LEN_1D/2`) uses `a_copy` for reverse accesses, so it sees original `a` values.  
- Second half (`i >= LEN_1D/2`) uses updated `a` for reverse accesses.

But let's test with a small example to see if this matches original semantics.

i=1: `x = a[2] + 2 = 30 + 2 = 32` → `a[1] = 31`, `b[1] = 32`  
i=2: `x = a[1] + 3` → **a[1] is now 31** → `31 + 3 = 34` → `a[2] = 33`, `b[2] = 34`  
i=3: `x = a[0] + 4` → **a[0] is now 40** → `40 + 4 = 44` → `a[3] = 43`, `b[3] = 44`

Final `a = [40, 31, 33, 43]`, `b = [41, 32, 34, 44]`.

Actually, the compiler's remark says: "Memory location is the same as accessed at line 23:23" — line 23 is `a[i] = x - 1.0f`. {

## **2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases**

The original loop accesses `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` in iteration `i`.  
In the original code, `a` is being updated in the same loop, so later iterations reading `a[LEN_1D - j - 1]` may read a value that has already been updated by an earlier iteration if `LEN_1D - j - 1` is less than `j`.  
This happens when `i < LEN_1D/2` roughly:  
- For `i = 0`, we read `a[LEN_1D - 1]` (original value).  
- For `i = LEN_1D - 1`, we read `a[0]`, which by then has been updated in iteration `i = 0`.

Thus, there is a **cross-iteration dependency** between `a[i]` and `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]`.

The optimized code tries to split the loop into two halves:  
- First half (`i < LEN_1D/2`) uses `a_copy` for reverse accesses, so it sees original `a` values.  
- Second half (`i >= LEN_1D/2`) uses updated `a` for reverse accesses.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

{

Optimized code:
```c

void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    float* a_copy = (float*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Copy original 'a' values for safe reverse access
        memcpy(a_copy, a, LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
        
        // Process first half: use original 'a' values from second half
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D/2; i++) {
            float x = a_copy[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Process second half: use updated 'a' values from first half
        for (int i = LEN_1D/2; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            float x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
    }
    
    free(a_copy);
}

```

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

Let’s go step by step.  

---

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**  
The optimized code uses `malloc` and `memcpy` but does not include `<stdlib.h>` or `<string.h>`.  
In C, missing includes would cause implicit function declaration warnings/errors depending on compiler flags.  
However, the compiler feedback shows it compiled (with remarks), so likely includes were added in actual testing.  
For this analysis, we assume it compiles without errors.  

---

**2. Semantic comparison**  

The source code loop for each `i` uses:  
```
x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
Here `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is **read** from the original `a` array at the **start** of the iteration for `i`.  
But `a[i]` is **written** in the same iteration.  

If `i` and `LEN_1D - i - 1` can be the same index (when `i == LEN_1D - i - 1`, i.e., `i == (LEN_1D - 1)/2` for odd length), then in the source code, the read happens **before** the write in program order, so it uses the old `a` value from before the loop iteration started.  

The optimized code copies the entire `a` into `a_original` **before** the inner loop, so all reads from `a_original` are from the state of `a` at the start of the inner loop.  

But in the source code, when `i` increases, `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` may refer to an index **already updated earlier** in the same inner loop if `LEN_1D - i - 1 < i`.  

Example with `LEN_1D = 3`:  

**Source code execution (first inner loop, nl=0):**  
- i=0: read a[2] (old), compute x, write a[0], b[0]  
- i=1: read a[1] (old), compute x, write a[1], b[1]  
- i=2: read a[0] (updated at i=0!), compute x, write a[2], b[2]  

**Optimized code execution (first inner loop, nl=0):**  
- i=0: read a_original[2] (old), compute x, write a[0], b[0]  
- i=1: read a_original[1] (old), compute x, write a[1], b[1]  
- i=2: read a_original[0] (old), compute x, write a[2], b[2]  

Difference: at i=2, source uses a[0] which was updated at i=0, but optimized uses original a[0].  

Thus semantics differ.  

---

**3. Step-by-step verification with small example**  

Let’s test with `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 1`, initial:  
a = [10, 20, 30], b = [1, 2, 3], c = [1, 1, 1]  

**Source:**  
i=0: x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0] = 30 + 1 = 31 → a[0]=30, b[0]=31  
i=1: x = a[1] + b[1]*c[1] = 20 + 2 = 22 → a[1]=21, b[1]=22  
i=2: x = a[0] + b[2]*c[2] = 30 + 3 = 33 → a[2]=32, b[2]=33  
Final a = [30, 21, 32], b = [31, 22, 33]  

**Optimized:**  
a_original = [10, 20, 30]  
i=0: x = 30 + 1 = 31 → a[0]=30, b[0]=31  
i=1: x = 20 + 2 = 22 → a[1]=21, b[1]=22  
i=2: x = 10 + 3 = 13 → a[2]=12, b[2]=13  
Final a = [30, 21, 12], b = [31, 22, 13]  

Different results → semantics mismatch.  

---

**4. Conclusion**  

The optimized code changes semantics because it fails to account for the **flow dependence** where `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` may be an element that was already overwritten earlier in the same inner loop when `LEN_1D - i - 1 < i`.  

Since the unit test analysis already indicates inconsistency, and our step-by-step confirms it, the optimized code is **incorrect**.

void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    float* a_original = (float*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Preserve original 'a' values for all reverse accesses
        memcpy(a_original, a, LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
        
        // Process all iterations using original 'a' values
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            float x = a_original[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
    }
    
    free(a_original);
}

```

Feedback:
Let’s go step by step.  

---

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**  
The optimized code uses `malloc` and `memcpy` but does not include `<stdlib.h>` or `<string.h>`.  
In C, missing includes would cause implicit function declaration warnings/errors depending on compiler flags.  
However, the compiler feedback shows it compiled (with remarks), so likely includes were added in actual testing.  
For this analysis, we assume it compiles without errors.  

---

**2. Semantic comparison**  

The source code loop for each `i` uses:  
```
x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
Here `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is **read** from the original `a` array at the **start** of the iteration for `i`.  
But `a[i]` is **written** in the same iteration.  

If `i` and `LEN_1D - i - 1` can be the same index (when `i == LEN_1D - i - 1`, i.e., `i == (LEN_1D - 1)/2` for odd length), then in the source code, the read happens **before** the write in program order, so it uses the old `a` value from before the loop iteration started.  

The optimized code copies the entire `a` into `a_original` **before** the inner loop, so all reads from `a_original` are from the state of `a` at the start of the inner loop.  

But in the source code, when `i` increases, `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` may refer to an index **already updated earlier** in the same inner loop if `LEN_1D - i - 1 < i`.  

Example with `LEN_1D = 3`:  

**Source code execution (first inner loop, nl=0):**  
- i=0: read a[2] (old), compute x, write a[0], b[0]  
- i=1: read a[1] (old), compute x, write a[1], b[1]  
- i=2: read a[0] (updated at i=0!), compute x, write a[2], b[2]  

**Optimized code execution (first inner loop, nl=0):**  
- i=0: read a_original[2] (old), compute x, write a[0], b[0]  
- i=1: read a_original[1] (old), compute x, write a[1], b[1]  
- i=2: read a_original[0] (old), compute x, write a[2], b[2]  

Difference: at i=2, source uses a[0] which was updated at i=0, but optimized uses original a[0].  

Thus semantics differ.  

---

**3. Step-by-step verification with small example**  

Let’s test with `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 1`, initial:  
a = [10, 20, 30], b = [1, 2, 3], c = [1, 1, 1]  

**Source:**  
i=0: x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0] = 30 + 1 = 31 → a[0]=30, b[0]=31  
i=1: x = a[1] + b[1]*c[1] = 20 + 2 = 22 → a[1]=21, b[1]=22  
i=2: x = a[0] + b[2]*c[2] = 30 + 3 = 33 → a[2]=32, b[2]=33  
Final a = [30, 21, 32], b = [31, 22, 33]  

**Optimized:**  
a_original = [10, 20, 30]  
i=0: x = 30 + 1 = 31 → a[0]=30, b[0]=31  
i=1: x = 20 + 2 = 22 → a[1]=21, b[1]=22  
i=2: x = 10 + 3 = 13 → a[2]=12, b[2]=13  
Final a = [30, 21, 12], b = [31, 22, 13]  

Different results → semantics mismatch.  

---

**4. Conclusion**  

The optimized code changes semantics because it fails to account for the **flow dependence** where `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` may be an element that was already overwritten earlier in the same inner loop when `LEN_1D - i - 1 < i`.  

Since the unit test analysis already indicates inconsistency, and our step-by-step confirms it, the optimized code is **incorrect**.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Split the loop into two halves to handle the reverse dependency
        int mid = LEN_1D / 2;
        
        // First half: i from 0 to mid-1
        // In this range, LEN_1D-i-1 >= mid, so a[LEN_1D-i-1] hasn't been written yet
        for (int i = 0; i < mid; i++) {
            float x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Handle middle element if LEN_1D is odd
        if (LEN_1D % 2 == 1) {
            int i = mid;
            float x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
            mid++; // Adjust mid for second half
        }
        
        // Second half: i from mid to LEN_1D-1
        // For these indices, LEN_1D-i-1 < i, so we need to use a temporary
        for (int i = mid; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            // Store the original value before it gets overwritten
            float orig_a = a[LEN_1D - i - 1];
            float x = orig_a + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
    }
}

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**FAIL** - The optimized code has incorrect semantics. Let me demonstrate with a simple example:

**Test Case:**
- LEN_1D = 3, iterations = 1
- a = [1.0, 2.0, 3.0]
- b = [4.0, 5.0, 6.0]
- c = [7.0, 8.0, 9.0]

**Original Code Execution:**
- i=0: x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0] = 3.0 + 4.0*7.0 = 31.0 → a[0]=30.0, b[0]=31.0
- i=1: x = a[1] + b[1]*c[1] = 2.0 + 5.0*8.0 = 42.0 → a[1]=41.0, b[1]=42.0
- i=2: x = a[0] + b[2]*c[2] = 30.0 + 6.0*9.0 = 84.0 → a[2]=83.0, b[2]=84.0

**Optimized Code Execution:**
- mid = 1
- First half (i=0): x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0] = 3.0 + 4.0*7.0 = 31.0 → a[0]=30.0, b[0]=31.0
- Middle element (i=1): x = a[1] + b[1]*c[1] = 2.0 + 5.0*8.0 = 42.0 → a[1]=41.0, b[1]=42.0
- Second half (i=2): orig_a = a[0] = 30.0, x = 30.0 + 6.0*9.0 = 84.0 → a[2]=83.0, b[2]=84.0

**The problem:** The optimized code incorrectly assumes that when processing the second half, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` has already been overwritten and needs to be saved. However, this is only true for some indices. In the example above:
- For i=2, LEN_1D-i-1 = 0, and a[0] was indeed overwritten in the first half.
- But for larger arrays, this assumption breaks down.

**Critical Issue:** The split point `mid = LEN_1D/2` doesn't correctly identify which `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` values have been overwritten. The correct condition is: `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` has been overwritten if `LEN_1D-i-1 < i`, which occurs when `i > LEN_1D/2`.

The optimized code's approach is fundamentally flawed because it attempts to split the loop based on a fixed midpoint, but the actual dependency pattern is more complex and depends on the relationship between `i` and `LEN_1D-i-1`.

**Semantic Difference:** The optimized code will produce different results than the original for many input cases, particularly when `LEN_1D` is large and the values in array `a` are not symmetric.

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

Looking at the original code, there's a loop-carried dependency through array `a` where `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is read and `a[i]` is written. When `i < LEN_1D-i-1`, we read from an unwritten location. When `i > LEN_1D-i-1`, we read from a location that may have been written earlier in the same iteration.

The key insight is that the dependency occurs only when `i > LEN_1D-i-1`, which simplifies to `i > (LEN_1D-1)/2`. So we can split the loop at this boundary.

```c
void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Process indices where i <= LEN_1D-i-1 (no dependency on current iteration's writes)
        int boundary = (LEN_1D) / 2;
        for (int i = 0; i < boundary; i++) {
            float x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Process indices where i > LEN_1D-i-1 (potential dependency)
        for (int i = boundary; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            // For i > boundary, LEN_1D-i-1 < i, so we need to save the original value
            float orig_a = a[LEN_1D - i - 1];
            float x = orig_a + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
    }
}

## Analysis Report  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code  
The optimized code compiles without errors. The compiler feedback shows only vectorization remarks, not compilation failures.  

---

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases  

The original loop iterates `i = 0` to `LEN_1D-1`, and in each iteration:  
- Accesses `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` (from the opposite end of the array).  
- Computes `x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i]`.  
- Updates `a[i] = x - 1.0f`.  
- Updates `b[i] = x`.  

The optimized code splits the loop into two parts:  
- **First half** (`i < boundary`): Here `LEN_1D-i-1 >= i`, so `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is from the untouched second half of `a` (from the previous iteration of `nl` loop). This is safe.  
- **Second half** (`i >= boundary`): Here `LEN_1D-i-1 < i`, so `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` might have been overwritten in the current `nl` iteration if `LEN_1D-i-1` falls in the first half. The optimized code saves `orig_a = a[LEN_1D-i-1]` to preserve the original value before the update.  

Let’s test with a small example:  

**Example 1**: `LEN_1D = 4`, initial `a = [10,20,30,40]`, `b = [1,2,3,4]`, `c = [1,1,1,1]`, `iterations = 1`.  

**Original**:  
- i=0: x = a[3] + b[0]*c[0] = 40 + 1 = 41 → a[0]=40, b[0]=41.  
- i=1: x = a[2] + 2 = 30 + 2 = 32 → a[1]=31, b[1]=32.  
- i=2: x = a[1] + 3 = 20 + 3 = 23 → a[2]=22, b[2]=23.  
- i=3: x = a[0] + 4 = 10 + 4 = 14 → a[3]=13, b[3]=14.  
Final a = [40, 31, 22, 13], b = [41, 32, 23, 14].  

**Optimized**: boundary = 2.  
First half i=0,1:  
- i=0: x = a[3]=40 + 1 = 41 → a[0]=40, b[0]=41.  
- i=1: x = a[2]=30 + 2 = 32 → a[1]=31, b[1]=32.  
Second half i=2,3:  
- i=2: orig_a = a[1] = 31 (already updated in first half! This is wrong — should be original a[1]=20).  
So x = 31 + 3 = 34 → a[2]=33, b[2]=34.  
- i=3: orig_a = a[0] = 40 (already updated in first half! Should be original a[0]=10).  
So x = 40 + 4 = 44 → a[3]=43, b[3]=44.  

Final a = [40, 31, 33, 43], b = [41, 32, 34, 44] → **Different from original**.  

**Conclusion**: The optimized code has a **semantic error** because in the second half, `orig_a = a[LEN_1D-i-1]` reads the already-updated value from the first half in the same `nl` iteration, but the original code reads the value from the start of the `nl` iteration.  

Thus, the optimization is **incorrect**.  

---

### 3. Vectorization Analysis  
Since semantics are incorrect, vectorization analysis is not required.  

---

**Final Result**: FAIL  
**Reason**: The optimized code changes the semantics of the original loop due to incorrect handling of dependencies when splitting the loop. The second half must use the original `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` values from before the current `nl` iteration, not the partially updated ones.

void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Save the original values of 'a' that will be read in reverse order

            float x = a_orig[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;

Let’s go step by step.  

---

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**  
The optimized code uses `__builtin_alloca(LEN_1D * sizeof(float))` inside the outer loop.  
- If `LEN_1D` is large, this could cause a stack overflow at runtime, but it’s not a compile-time error.  
- However, `__builtin_alloca` is a GCC/Clang extension, not standard C, so it may not compile on all compilers, but the feedback says it compiled (with remarks), so we assume no compilation error here.  

---

**2. Semantic comparison**  

The original loop for a given `nl`:  
```c
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
    x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
    a[i] = x - 1.0f;
    b[i] = x;
}
```

Key observation:  
- `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is read **before** `a[i]` is written in the same iteration.  
- But `a[i]` may have been overwritten in a **previous iteration** of the same `i` loop if `LEN_1D-i-1 == i` or if `LEN_1D-i-1` is less than `i` and already processed? Let’s check:  

Example: `LEN_1D = 5`  

Iteration `i=0`: reads `a[4]` (original from start of this `nl` iteration), writes `a[0]`.  
`i=1`: reads `a[3]` (original), writes `a[1]`.  
`i=2`: reads `a[2]` (original), writes `a[2]` — here `a[2]` is read then immediately overwritten.  
`i=3`: reads `a[1]` — but `a[1]` was already overwritten at `i=1` in this same `i` loop!  
So the original code has a **loop-carried dependency**: `a[1]` is modified at `i=1`, then read at `i=3`.  

Thus, reading `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` after it might have been overwritten in an earlier iteration of the same `i` loop is **intentional** in the original code.  

---

**Optimized code behavior**:  
It first copies `a` to `a_orig`, then uses `a_orig[LEN_1D-i-1]` for the read.  
This means it always reads the original `a` from the start of the `nl` iteration, **not** the updated `a` from earlier `i` in the same `i` loop.  

This changes semantics.  

---

**Example to show difference**:  
Let `LEN_1D = 3`, `a = [10,20,30]`, `b = [1,1,1]`, `c = [2,2,2]`, `iterations=1`.  

**Original**:  
`i=0`: `x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0] = 30 + 2 = 32`, `a[0]=31`, `b[0]=32`.  
`i=1`: `x = a[1] + b[1]*c[1] = 20 + 2 = 22`, `a[1]=21`, `b[1]=22`.  
`i=2`: `x = a[0] + b[2]*c[2] = 31 + 2 = 33`, `a[2]=32`, `b[2]=33`.  
Final `a = [31,21,32]`.  

**Optimized**:  
Copy `a_orig = [10,20,30]`.  
`i=0`: `x = a_orig[2] + 2 = 30+2=32`, `a[0]=31`, `b[0]=32`.  
`i=1`: `x = a_orig[1] + 2 = 20+2=22`, `a[1]=21`, `b[1]=22`.  
`i=2`: `x = a_orig[0] + 2 = 10+2=12`, `a[2]=11`, `b[2]=12`.  
Final `a = [31,21,11]`.  

Different results → semantics are inconsistent.  

---

**3. Conclusion**  
The optimized code changes the meaning by breaking the loop-carried dependency that existed in the original.  
Thus, it fails the semantic equivalence test.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Split the loop into two halves to handle the reverse dependency
        int mid = LEN_1D / 2;
        
        // First half: process indices where LEN_1D-i-1 >= i
        // This ensures we read a[LEN_1D-i-1] before it's overwritten
        for (int i = 0; i < mid; i++) {
            float x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Handle middle element separately if LEN_1D is odd
        if (LEN_1D % 2 == 1) {
            int i = mid;
            float x = a[mid] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Second half: process indices where LEN_1D-i-1 < i
        // These read from a elements that were already updated in first half
        for (int i = mid + (LEN_1D % 2); i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            float x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
    }
}

In the original, when `i` runs from `0` to `LEN_1D-1`, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is read before `a[i]` is written, except when `LEN_1D-i-1 == i` (middle element for odd length).

The optimized code splits into two halves:

- Second half `i = mid..LEN_1D-1`: reads `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` from first half of array, which was already updated in the first half of this iteration.

In original, iteration `i` reads `a[LEN_1D-i-1]`, which is updated in iteration `LEN_1D-i-1`.  
If `i > LEN_1D-i-1`, then that update happened earlier in the same loop pass.

void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Split the loop into two halves to handle the reverse dependency
        int mid = LEN_1D / 2;
        
        // First half: process indices where LEN_1D-i-1 >= i
        // This ensures we read a[LEN_1D-i-1] before it's overwritten
        for (int i = 0; i < mid; i++) {
            float x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Handle middle element separately if LEN_1D is odd
        if (LEN_1D % 2 == 1) {
            int i = mid;
            float x = a[mid] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
        
        // Second half: process indices where LEN_1D-i-1 < i
        // These read from a elements that were already updated in first half
        for (int i = mid + (LEN_1D % 2); i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            float x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];

**Preservation Requirements**:

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**2. Semantic comparison**  

The source code loop for each iteration `nl` does:  

```c
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
    x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
    a[i] = x - 1.0f;
    b[i] = x;
}
```

The key is `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` — it reads from the **original** `a` array at the **mirrored index** from the end, but `a[i]` is being overwritten earlier in the same iteration for smaller `i`.  

In the source code, when `i` is small, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is near the end of the array, which hasn’t been overwritten yet in this `nl` iteration (because we write only to `a[i]`, not `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` unless `i` is large enough that `LEN_1D-i-1 <= i`).  
Actually, let’s check:  
If `LEN_1D = 5`,  
- `i=0`: read `a[4]` (unchanged so far in this nl iteration), write `a[0]`  
- `i=1`: read `a[3]` (unchanged so far), write `a[1]`  
- `i=2`: read `a[2]` (unchanged so far), write `a[2]` — here read and write same index, but read happens before write in this iteration, so reads old value from previous iteration or earlier in this iteration? Wait, `a[2]` not written yet in this nl iteration, so reads old value from start of nl iteration.  
- `i=3`: read `a[1]` — but `a[1]` was written at `i=1` in this same nl iteration! So it reads the **updated** `a[1]` from earlier in the loop.  
- `i=4`: read `a[0]` — `a[0]` was written at `i=0` in this same nl iteration.  

So there is a **loop-carried dependency** within the same `nl` iteration:  
For `i >= ceil(LEN_1D/2)`, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is an index that has already been written earlier in this same `nl` iteration.  

Thus the source code uses **partially updated `a`** for the second half of the loop.

---

The optimized code copies `a` to `a_old` at the start of each `nl` iteration, then always reads from `a_old[LEN_1D-i-1]`, which is the original `a` from the **start** of the `nl` iteration, not partially updated values.  

This changes semantics for indices where `LEN_1D-i-1 < i` (i.e., `i > (LEN_1D-1)/2`).

---
