Source code: 
```c
void s281(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a,float* b,float* c)
{
    float x;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x-(float)1.0;
            b[i] = x;
        }
    }
}
```

Optimized code:
```c

void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    // Allocate temporary array to store original 'a' values
    float* a_old = (float*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Copy original 'a' to temporary array
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            a_old[i] = a[i];
        }
        
        // Process all iterations in a single vectorizable loop
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            float x = a_old[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
            a[i] = x - 1.0f;
            b[i] = x;
        }
    }
    
    free(a_old);
```
```c
}

```

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
Let’s go step by step.

---

### **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**
The optimized code uses `__builtin_alloca` inside the outer loop, allocating a variable-length array on the stack each iteration.  
While this compiles in GCC/Clang, it’s not standard C and could cause stack overflow if `LEN_1D` is large.  
But the compiler feedback shows it compiled without errors for the test, so we proceed.

---

### **2. Semantic comparison**

**Source code semantics:**
In the original loop, for each `i`:
```
x = a[LEN_1D - i - 1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
Here, `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]` is read from the **original** `a` array (before any updates in the current iteration), because updates to `a[i]` only affect indices `0..i-1` in the same iteration, and `LEN_1D - i - 1` is ≥ `i` for `i ≤ LEN_1D/2`, but for `i > LEN_1D/2`, `LEN_1D - i - 1` < `i`, meaning later iterations read from `a` elements that have already been updated earlier in the same iteration — this is a **loop-carried dependency**.

Example: `LEN_1D = 3`, iteration order:

- `i=0`: reads `a[2]` (original), writes `a[0]`
- `i=1`: reads `a[1]` (original), writes `a[1]`
- `i=2`: reads `a[0]` (already updated in `i=0`!), writes `a[2]`

So the source code uses **updated** `a[0]` when computing `i=2`.

---

**Optimized code semantics:**
It first copies `a` in reverse into `a_rev`, so `a_rev[i] = original a[LEN_1D - i - 1]`.  
Then in the second loop:
```
x = a_rev[i] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
Now `a_rev[i]` is **always** the original `a[LEN_1D - i - 1]`, never the updated one.  
This differs from the source code when `LEN_1D - i - 1 < i`.

---

**Test case to show difference:**
Let `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 1`,  
`a = [10, 20, 30]`, `b = [1, 1, 1]`, `c = [2, 2, 2]`.

**Source code:**
- `i=0`: `x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0] = 30 + 2 = 32` → `a[0]=31`, `b[0]=32`
- `i=1`: `x = a[1] + 2 = 20 + 2 = 22` → `a[1]=21`, `b[1]=22`
- `i=2`: `x = a[0] + 2 = 31 + 2 = 33` → `a[2]=32`, `b[2]=33`
Final `a = [31, 21, 32]`, `b = [32, 22, 33]`

**Optimized code:**
`a_rev = [30, 20, 10]`
- `i=0`: `x = a_rev[0] + 2 = 30 + 2 = 32` → `a[0]=31`, `b[0]=32`
- `i=1`: `x = a_rev[1] + 2 = 20 + 2 = 22` → `a[1]=21`, `b[1]=22`
- `i=2`: `x = a_rev[2] + 2 = 10 + 2 = 12` → `a[2]=11`, `b[2]=12`
Final `a = [31, 21, 11]`, `b = [32, 22, 12]`

Different results → **semantics mismatch**.

---

### **3. Conclusion**
The optimized code changed the dependency pattern, breaking correctness.  
Since semantics are wrong, we stop here.

---

If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

Actually, the problem is more subtle. The issue is that when `i` accesses `a[LEN_1D-i-1]`, for indices in the second half, it might read values from the first half that have already been modified in the current iteration. However, in the original code, all reads of `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` happen before any writes to those positions in the same iteration.

Let me trace with `LEN_1D=4`:

**Original code**:
- i=0: reads a[3], writes a[0]
- i=1: reads a[2], writes a[1]  
- i=2: reads a[1], writes a[2] ← reads a[1] which was just written at i=1!
- i=3: reads a[0], writes a[3] ← reads a[0] which was written at i=0!

**Optimized code** (mid=2):
- First half (i=0,1): reads original a[3], a[2], writes a[0], a[1]
- Second half (i=2): reads a[1] (which was just written!), writes a[2]
- Second half (i=3): reads a[0] (which was written at i=0), writes a[3]

This is actually **correct**! The optimized code preserves the dependencies because:
1. When i < LEN_1D/2, we read from the second half of array `a` (which hasn't been modified yet in this iteration)
2. When i ≥ LEN_1D/2, we read from the first half of array `a` (which has already been modified in this iteration)

The compiler's vectorization warning is about potential dependencies, but semantically the code is correct. The unit test also passed.

### 3. Vectorization Analysis

The compiler feedback shows the loops are not vectorized due to "unsafe dependent memory operations". The issue is that there's a true dependency:
- `a[i] = x - 1.0f` (write to a[i])
- Later iterations might read `a[LEN_1D-j-1]` where `LEN_1D-j-1 = i`

This is a loop-carried anti-dependency that prevents vectorization. The optimized code's loop splitting doesn't eliminate this dependency; it just separates it into two loops.

**Why vectorization fails**:
1. In the first half-loop: writes to `a[i]` (low indices) don't affect reads of `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` (high indices)
2. In the second half-loop: reads of `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` (low indices) depend on writes from the first half-loop

The dependency pattern is complex and prevents automatic vectorization.

**Optimization opportunity**: The code could be made vectorization-friendly by:
1. Using temporary arrays to break dependencies
2. Applying loop distribution to separate reads from writes
3. Using SIMD intrinsics for manual vectorization

Since there's an optimization opportunity (the code is not vectorized and could be improved), and the analysis shows potential for better optimization, this should be marked as **FAIL**.

```c
void s281_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
```
        // Temporary array to store original values of a needed for the second half
        float* temp_a = (float*)alloca(LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
        
        // First: compute all x values and store them in b temporarily
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            temp_a[i] = a[LEN_1D-i-1];  // Save original values
            float x = temp_a[i] + b[i] * c[i];

Let’s go step-by-step.

---

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**  
The optimized code uses `alloca(LEN_1D * sizeof(float))` inside the loop over `nl`.  
`alloca` allocates on the stack, but if `LEN_1D` is large, this could cause stack overflow.  
However, the code compiles without syntax errors, so we proceed.

---

**2. Semantic comparison**  

**Source code behavior** (per iteration `nl`):  
For `i = 0 .. LEN_1D-1`:
```
x = a[LEN_1D-i-1] + b[i] * c[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
Important: `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is **read** from the original `a` array (as it was at the start of the iteration over `i`), but `a[i]` is **written** later.  
This means for `i` and `j` where `j = LEN_1D-i-1`, there is a **true dependency** if `j > i` (because `a[j]` is read for computing `x` for index `i`, but `a[j]` itself is overwritten when loop reaches `i = j`).  
Specifically:  
- For first half of loop (`i < LEN_1D/2`), `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is from second half of array, not yet overwritten.  
- For second half (`i > LEN_1D/2`), `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is from first half of array, which **has already been overwritten** in this same iteration over `i`.  
Thus, the source code uses **updated `a` values** for the second half of the array in the same loop iteration.

**Optimized code behavior**:  
First loop:
```
temp_a[i] = a[LEN_1D-i-1];  // save original a values
float x = temp_a[i] + b[i] * c[i];
b[i] = x;
```
Second loop:
```
float x = b[i];
a[i] = x - 1.0f;
b[i] = x;
```
This uses **original** `a` values for **all** `i`, because `temp_a[i]` captured `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` before any `a[i]` was overwritten.  
But in source code, for second half of indices, `a[LEN_1D-i-1]` is already the **new** value (from earlier in the same iteration).  
Thus, semantics differ.

```
i=2: x = a[0] + b[2]*c[2]; a[2]=x-1; b[2]=x;
```
Note: at i=2, a[0] is already updated from i=0 step.

Optimized:
First loop saves temp_a = [a[2], a[1], a[0]].
i=0: x = a[2] + b[0]*c[0]; b[0]=x;
i=1: x = a[1] + b[1]*c[1]; b[1]=x;
i=2: x = a[0] + b[2]*c[2]; b[2]=x;

i=1: a[1] = b[1]-1; b[1]=b[1];
i=2: a[2] = b[2]-1; b[2]=b[2];

Difference: In source, i=2 uses new a[0] (from i=0). In optimized, i=2 uses original a[0] (saved in temp_a[2]).

Thus, semantics are **inconsistent**.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Reason**: The loop splitting approach is flawed due to a critical dependency pattern. Let's analyze with a concrete example:

**Example 1** (LEN_1D = 4, iterations = 1):
- Source code processes sequentially: i=0,1,2,3
- Optimized code processes: i=0,1 (first half), then i=2,3 (second half)

**Step-by-step comparison**:

**Source code execution**:
- i=0: `x = a[3] + b[0]*c[0]` → uses original a[3]
- i=1: `x = a[2] + b[1]*c[1]` → uses original a[2]
- i=2: `x = a[1] + b[2]*c[2]` → uses original a[1]
- i=3: `x = a[0] + b[3]*c[3]` → uses original a[0]

**Optimized code execution**:
- First half (i=0,1):
  - i=0: `x = a[3] + b[0]*c[0]` → uses original a[3]
  - i=1: `x = a[2] + b[1]*c[1]` → uses original a[2]
- Second half (i=2,3):

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

     ```c
    else if (cond2)

    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

### **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**

If `LEN_1D` is large, this could cause a stack overflow, but it’s not a compilation error in C99 or GCC with VLAs enabled. However, the compiler feedback is from a C++ file (`temp_code.cpp`), and VLAs are not standard in C++ (though some compilers support them as an extension).

The optimized version stores `a` into `temp_a` at the start of the `nl` iteration, so `temp_a[LEN_1D-i-1]` equals original `a[LEN_1D-i-1]`. This matches the original’s requirement.
